     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), and find that 31\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\%) of the instability; 92.4\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation; cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

   - `paper/sections/introduction.tex`
     - P3: Novelty repositioned as "empirical and methodological, not algorithmic"
     - P0: Claim 4 reframed around z-scores + Jaccard + difficulty interaction (GSM1K removed)

     - Key paragraph (line 19):
     ```latex
     The MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale characterization of item-level benchmark stability at 5,227 models, finding 31\% temporal instability that is robust across thresholds and unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains; (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:
     ```
     - Claim 4 (line 28):
     ```latex
     \item \textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\Cref{sec:downstream}).
     ```

     ```latex
     The Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}.
     ```

 format

     ```latex
     \subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
     \label{sec:disc_protocol}
     
     These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\% $> 20\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).
     ```

     ```latex
     \section{Conclusion}
     \label{sec:conclusion}
     
     Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), we find that 31\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification. The instability is robust across thresholds (10--49\% at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in [2.5, 1.0]$), confirmed non-random by a random-split control (0.18\%), and predominantly item-specific (92.4\%). This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift. Web-overlap contamination labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\approx 0.96$), exposing a validation methodology gap; our semi-synthetic framework with cross-subject matching ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative, confirmed on GSM8K.
     
     DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal, Jaccard with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode. DIF is a diagnostic lens, not a quality filter. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring---periodic DIF audits, item-level stability labels, and cross-subject matching---alongside score reporting.
     ```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$); ability-matched C\\% of $28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$ confirms the 31.3\\% rate is not inflated by impact confounding. Domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 pp of 31.3\\% C\\%; 92.4\\% is item-specific. This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: the dominant validation practice of evaluating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items. This near-independence is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization. Models with larger contamination gaps benefit \\emph{less} from DIF-C items. Of the 249 DIF-C items on GSM8K, 171 favor the reference (2023) cohort ($\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} > 1$). Partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms the effect is not an artifact of general model quality. Caveats include a wide confidence interval (95\\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$) and limited sample size.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.\n\nStructural constraints and limitations are discussed in the Limitations section.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).\n",
        "numLines": 29,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 29
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/discussion.tex"
  },
```

```json
  "isSidechain": false,

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nApplying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), we find that 31\\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification. The instability is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$), confirmed non-random by a random-split control (0.18\\%), and predominantly item-specific (92.4\\%). This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift. Web-overlap contamination labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$), exposing a validation methodology gap; our semi-synthetic framework with cross-subject matching ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative, confirmed on GSM8K.\n\nDIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode. DIF is a diagnostic lens, not a quality filter. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring---periodic DIF audits, item-level stability labels, and cross-subject matching---alongside score reporting.\n",
        "numLines": 7,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 7
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/conclusion.tex"
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "d167323c-7124-4c4e-a8c1-12a10dcedfcf",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological scope of MH-DIF.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure detects only \\emph{uniform} DIF, where the odds ratio between cohorts is constant across ability strata. Logistic regression DIF analysis reveals an additional 24.8\\% of items exhibiting non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% ETS~C estimate conservative. Future work should apply non-uniform DIF methods (e.g., logistic regression or IRT-based approaches) as the primary analysis to capture the full spectrum of item instability. Critically, MH-DIF is nonparametric and conditions on observed total score, not latent $\\theta$---its validity does not depend on IRT model fit~\\citep{holland1988mh}. Our Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence domains do not correlate with DIF domains ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), further supporting the robustness of the MH procedure in this setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Ability confounding.}\nAlthough the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible (Cohen's $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$) and nearest-neighbor matching confirms that at most 2.4 percentage points of C\\% are attributable to ability mismatch, within-family comparisons with large ability gaps (e.g., Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3, $d = -0.99$) produce substantially inflated C\\% (61.3\\% unmatched vs.\\ 28.3\\% matched). Any application of MH-DIF to cohorts with larger ability differences must incorporate ability matching or stratification beyond the standard 5-strata procedure.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size requirements.}\nOur power analysis indicates reliable ETS~C detection requires $N \\geq 80$ per cohort. The temporal cohorts (1,080 and 807 models) far exceed this threshold, but the GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power (95\\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$). The negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$) is exploratory and warrants replication with larger model samples.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination vs.\\ capability change.}\nDIF analysis detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts but cannot determine \\emph{why}. The differential signal may reflect contamination, capability change, training methodology shifts, or interactions among these factors. We present evidence that DIF captures something distinct from memorization (negative GSM1K correlation, semi-synthetic dose-response), but a definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data.\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark scope.}\nWe analyze MMLU (12,508 items) and GSM8K (1,319 items), both multiple-choice or short-answer benchmarks scored as binary correct/incorrect. The generalizability of our findings to open-ended generation benchmarks (e.g., AlpacaEval, MT-Bench), coding benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval), or benchmarks with non-binary scoring remains untested. The binary response format is a prerequisite for MH-DIF; extending DIF analysis to continuous or partial-credit scoring would require ordinal or polytomous DIF methods.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal cohort definition.}\nWe define cohorts by calendar year of model release (2023 vs.\\ 2024), a coarse grouping that aggregates models with diverse training pipelines, data mixtures, and post-training procedures. Finer-grained cohort definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff, model family, or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns of item instability but would reduce per-cohort sample sizes and statistical power.\n",
        "numLines": 20,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 20
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/limitations.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "29ea08d7-b77a-472b-b4b0-3cab1383cf5d",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\\% \\citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \\citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies: ConStat \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} tests performance gaps between original and perturbed items, DVD \\citep{liang2026dvd} uses distributional verification, and Self-Critique \\citep{tao2026selfcritique} employs model self-evaluation. \\citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \\citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (Claim~2, \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, and scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which addresses score comparability through fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work is complementary: Growing Pains assumes anchor item stability, while DIF analysis diagnoses which items violate this assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{zumbo1999handbook} provides a comprehensive comparison. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The scale of this analysis (5,227 examinees across a 57-subject multidimensional ability space) exceeds typical educational testing applications, where \\citet{belzak2020smallsample} note that DIF methods can behave differently at extreme sample sizes. The primary methodological challenge is the impact-versus-DIF confound: genuine ability differences between temporal cohorts can inflate DIF estimates. We address this through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Design and Evaluation.}\nDynamic benchmarks such as Dynabench \\citep{kiela2021dynabench}, LatestEval \\citep{li2024latesteval}, and LiveBench \\citep{white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Chatbot Arena \\citep{chiang2024chatbot} sidesteps static benchmarks through pairwise preference. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and lack of item-level transparency as systemic challenges. Our DIF analysis fills this gap by identifying which items have become unreliable for cross-generation comparison. METABENCH \\citep{kipnis2024metabench} provides the large-scale response matrix that makes this analysis possible.\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/related_work.tex"
  },
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. **新数据**：
```

plan_028 — 6/6 METABENCH benchmarks 的 temporal DIF 结果：
| Benchmark | Items | C% | Random C% | Ratio |
|-----------|-------|----|-----------|-------|
| MMLU | 12,508 | 31.3% | 0.18% | 174× |
| WinoGrande | 1,267 | 30.3% | 0.32% | 95× |
| TruthfulQA | 817 | 29.7% | 0.37% | 80× |
| ARC | 1,172 | 26.9% | 0.60% | 45× |
| GSM8K | 1,319 | 18.9% | 0.30% | 63× |
| HellaSwag | 10,042 | 17.9% | 0.28% | 64× |

关键发现：知识/推理类 (MMLU, TruthfulQA, ARC, WinoGrande) C%=26.9-31.3%；数学/常识类 (GSM8K, HellaSwag) C%=17.9-18.9%。

plan_030 — Odds ratio regression 发现 Simpson's paradox：控制 item properties 后 accuracy change 的 OR 反转。

**修改要求**：
1. §4 Results 新增 cross-benchmark summary table（上面的 6-row table）

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ERE9K6SLWaxzyCXpnui33m`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	A power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.
10	
11	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Item Instability}
12	\label{sec:temporal}
13	
14	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
15	
16	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\%. Crucially, the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ (below the $|d| = 0.2$ small-effect threshold) with 76.4\% distributional overlap. Under strict 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$---a 7.7\% relative reduction from 31.3\%, confirming that at most $\sim$2.4 pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch.
17	
18	Across all 57 MMLU subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\% as a robustness check (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\% C\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\% $= 48.9\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.0$, $18.3\%$ at $\geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$.
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}
23	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
24	\small
25	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
26	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
27	\toprule
28	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\
29	\midrule
30	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
31	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
32	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
33	Random split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\
34	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
40	\label{sec:groundtruth}
41	
42	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\phi = 0.012$, $\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.
43	
44	\begin{figure}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
47	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
48	\label{fig:dose_response}
49	\end{figure}
50	
51	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
52	\label{sec:structural}
53	
54	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\% at $|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), making the 31\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\% vs.\ random 26.8\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\% vs.\ 30.9\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\% \pm 1.6\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$), while temporal matching reduces C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$ ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.76$). The matched C\% values ($\sim$28--29\%) are consistent with each other despite vastly different unmatched rates (61.3\% vs.\ 31.3\%), suggesting that genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items, with the Llama comparison carrying 33 pp of additional ability confound (54\% reduction) versus only 2.4 pp for temporal cohorts (7.7\% reduction). Full details in \Cref{app:structural}.
55	
56	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
57	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
58	
59	The critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\Cref{app:fig_matching}).
60	
61	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
62	\label{sec:downstream}
63	
64	Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\% of models shift by $|\Delta\mathrm{Rank}| \geq 100$. The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.
65	
66	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}
67	\label{sec:gsm8k}
68	
69	Replication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\% $= 18.9\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\%), with semi-synthetic $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.
70	
71	\begin{figure}[t]
72	\centering
73	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}
74	\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}
75	\label{fig:gsm1k}
76	\end{figure}
77	
```

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), and find that 31\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\%) of the instability; 92.4\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation; cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\% range 21--48\%, unweighted mean $\approx 30\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\rho \approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.
5	
6	A growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\%~\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}
11	\caption{Global vs.\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}
12	\label{fig:global_vs_differential}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	We apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about MMLU's measurement properties.
16	
17	MMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\rho \approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\% of models shift by $|\Delta\mathrm{Rank}| \geq 100$.
18	
19	The MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale characterization of item-level benchmark stability at 5,227 models, finding 31\% temporal instability that is robust across thresholds and unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains; (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:
20	
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	\item \textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification, with C\% ranging from 21\% to 48\% across all 57 subjects (mean $\approx 30\%$) and confirmed non-random by random-split control at 0.18\% (\Cref{sec:temporal}).
23	
24	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
25	
26	\item \textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).
27	
28	\item \textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\Cref{sec:downstream}).
29	\end{enumerate}
30
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have detailed metrics from both experiments. Let me also read the detailed reports for more context.

plan_028 metrics:
- MMLU: 12508 items, C%=31.3, random=0.18, ratio=174×
- WinoGrande: 1267 items, C%=30.3, random=0.32, ratio=95×
- TruthfulQA: 817 items, C%=29.7, random=0.37, ratio=80×
- ARC: 1172 items, C%=26.9, random=0.60, ratio=45×


- GSM8K: 1319 items, C%=18.9, random=0.30, ratio=63×
- HellaSwag: 10042 items, C%=17.9, random=0.28, ratio=64×

For plan_030, I'm seeing Simpson's paradox at work—when I include domain delta alone, the odds ratio shows a strong positive effect of 1.55 per 0.10 increase, but once I add item properties to the model, that relationship flips to 0.73, suggesting the item characteristics are confounding the original association.

The same reversal happens with item delta: starting at 1.08, then dropping to 0.92 when I control for difficulty and discrimination. Item difficulty itself shows easier items are more likely to exhibit DIF (OR=0.77-0.78), while more discriminating items are more likely to show DIF (OR=1.51-1.54). The likelihood ratio tests confirm item properties are highly significant predictors—χ²=647 and χ²=656 respectively—so the paradox is real: accuracy changes predict DIF at the aggregate level, but that relationship reverses once I account for the underlying item characteristics.

Now I'm mapping out the edits needed across the paper. I need to restructure the cross-benchmark validation section with a 6-benchmark table and differentiate by type, add a paragraph on Simpson's paradox, update the abstract and introduction to reflect the expanded benchmark set, and revise the discussion and conclusion sections on generalizability. Let me work through these systematically.

For the Simpson's paradox discussion, I'm deciding between integrating it into §4.3 (Structural Analysis) or placing it as a new subsection. Since §4.3 already covers structural controls that fail to reduce C% below 31%, and the Simpson's paradox is really about the relationship between accuracy change and DIF driven by item properties, it fits better as a continuation of the item-level regression results in §4.2 (Temporal Comparability). That's where the foundation for this argument already exists.

So I'm revising my approach: extend the existing logistic regression paragraph in §4.2 with the Simpson's paradox finding, then restructure §4.6 into "Cross-Benchmark Validation" with a 6-benchmark table, update the reference in §4.5, revise the opening scope statement, and cascade updates through the abstract, introduction, discussion, and conclusion.

Now I'm working through the page budget. Adding the table, its discussion paragraph, and Simpson's paradox sentences comes to roughly 0.4 pages of additional content. The key is that the cross-benchmark table actually replaces some existing text in §4.6 that currently discusses individual results, so there's built-in compression. If I also move the GSM1K figure to the appendix or trim that section, I should have enough room to fit everything without exceeding the page limit.

Let me just make these edits and compile to see where we land. I also need to verify which benchmark citations are already in the bibliography—WinoGrande, TruthfulQA, ARC, and HellaSwag—so I don't add duplicates.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the data I need. Let me also read the end of references.bib to know where to append the new entries.

Let me summarize the key numbers for plan_030:
- Model 1: domain accuracy delta → OR=1.55 per 0.10 [1.19, 2.03] (positive association)
- Model 2: + item props → OR=0.73 per 0.10 [0.55, 0.97] (REVERSES! Simpson's paradox)
- Item difficulty: OR=0.78 per 0.10 (easier → more likely DIF)


- Item discrimination: OR=1.51 per 0.10 (more discriminating → more likely DIF)
- LR test M1 vs M2: χ²=647.0 (item props add massive explanatory power)

The core finding is that at the domain level, subjects with larger accuracy improvements show more DIF items, but this relationship flips once I account for item difficulty and discrimination. The reversal happens because DIF items tend to be harder and more discriminating, and those properties cluster in subjects that improved more—a classic confounding pattern. Now I need to plan the edits carefully given the page constraints.

Let me check the end of the references file and then work through all the revisions systematically.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01W8e84rxMQxCjch1JGWeNC3`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method Details}
2	\label{app:method_details}
3	
4	\subsection{Diagnostics}
5	\label{app:diagnostics}
6	
7	Three diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.
8	
9	\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.
10	
11	\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\rho$ between subject-level C\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.
12	
13	\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.
14	
15	\subsection{Statistical Considerations}
16	\label{app:statistical}
17	
18	\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.
19	
20	\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in \{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.
21	
22	\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.
23	
24	\section{Structural Analysis Details}
25	\label{app:structural}
26	
27	Three progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.
28	
29	The temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject; $5{\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).
30	
31	\begin{table}[h]
32	\centering
33	\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces baseline FPR below 31\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching. Subscript $\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 injection seeds (baseline row only).}
34	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
35	\small
36	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
37	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
38	\toprule
39	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ FPR & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
40	\midrule
```

```text
8. **discussion.tex**: Update generalizability discussion

9. **conclusion.tex**: Update generalizability claim

Now I'll execute these changes, starting with the major revision to experiments.tex §4.6.

I'm rewriting the GSM8K section to present the replication results more concisely: showing the temporal contamination rate of 18.9% with the sanity check, the semi-synthetic effect size, and then pivoting to the GSM1K validation analysis which reveals a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage—a finding that actually contradicts the initial hypothesis about DIF items benefiting from contamination. The temporal DIF pattern holds across all six benchmarks, with contamination percentages ranging from 17.9% to 31.3%—each substantially higher than random baselines. I'm noticing a clear split: knowledge-focused tasks like MMLU and WinoGrande show higher contamination rates around 27-31%, while procedural tasks like GSM8K and HellaSwag cluster lower around 18-19%, suggesting that knowledge-intensive items are more susceptible to temporal drift.

Generating benchmark table... Now I'm looking at the GSM8K validation results more closely. The semi-synthetic injection approach shows a strong detection effect, and I'm cross-checking this against the GSM1K held-out set to see if contamination actually helps or hurts performance on these items. The correlation analysis suggests contaminated models don't gain an advantage on these items, which goes against what I'd expect if they were just memorizing answers.

For the Simpson's paradox section, I'm diving into the item-level regression to understand why domain-level accuracy improvements explain so little of the overall instability. The odds ratio analysis is revealing something counterintuitive about how the paradox manifests. The raw odds ratio of 80.86 reflects the full 0-1 scale of the accuracy improvement variable, so expressing it per 0.10 (10 percentage points) gives a more interpretable effect size of 1.55. In the adjusted model, item discrimination emerges as the dominant predictor at 1.51 per 0.10, while the accuracy improvement effect flips to 0.73, revealing a classic Simpson's paradox where the aggregate association masks the true conditional relationship. I'm revising the downstream impact section to generalize beyond just GSM8K—the instability pattern should be framed as extending across all six METABENCH benchmarks rather than treating GSM8K as a single follow-up. I'll update both the closing sentence of section 4.5 and the experiments introduction to reflect this broader validation story.

For the abstract, I need to be careful about space constraints while still conveying that the DIF instability generalizes across the full benchmark suite. The current flow focuses on MMLU with GSM8K as confirmation, but I should weave in that this pattern holds consistently across all six benchmarks with the reported C% ranges and baseline comparisons. I'm identifying the key places in the introduction where I need to update references from GSM8K alone to the full six-benchmark results—specifically in the contribution statements and the opening paragraphs that describe the replication scope. I'm going to start making the edits systematically, beginning with the experiments section since that's where the most substantial changes need to happen. I need to carefully match the old text in the paper to replace it with the updated version that expands the scope from MMLU to the six benchmarks.
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\\times$ above its random-split baseline---none attributable to sampling noise. A task-type dichotomy emerges: knowledge and reasoning benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande~\\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$, while procedural benchmarks (GSM8K, HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) show C\\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\\%$, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to shifts in training data composition across model generations.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. All six show substantial instability (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.18 & 174$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn GSM8K specifically, semi-synthetic injection with external matching confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) shows a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$), suggesting DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k})."
```

```json
    "oldString": "\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}).     "newString": "\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) shows a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$), suggesting DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\nA power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Item Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Crucially, the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ (below the $|d| = 0.2$ small-effect threshold) with 76.4\\% distributional overlap. Under strict 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \\approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$---a 7.7\\% relative reduction from 31.3\\%, confirming that at most $\\sim$2.4 pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch.\n\nAcross all 57 MMLU subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\\% as a robustness check (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\\phi = 0.012$, $\\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\% at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\% vs.\\ random 26.8\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\% vs.\\ 30.9\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% to $28.3\\% \\pm 1.6\\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$), while temporal matching reduces C\\% from 31.3\\% to $28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$ ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.76$). The matched C\\% values ($\\sim$28--29\\%) are consistent with each other despite vastly different unmatched rates (61.3\\% vs.\\ 31.3\\%), suggesting that genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\\sim$28--29\\% of items, with the Llama comparison carrying 33 pp of additional ability confound (54\\% reduction) versus only 2.4 pp for temporal cohorts (7.7\\% reduction). Full details in \\Cref{app:structural}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$. The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n",
```

```json
    "newString": "Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. Odds ratio regression reveals a Simpson's paradox: domain accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$ per 10 pp, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$), confirming that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification.",
    "originalFile": null,

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Crucially, the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ (below the $|d| = 0.2$ small-effect threshold) with 76.4\\% distributional overlap. Under strict 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \\approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$---a 7.7\\% relative reduction from 31.3\\%, confirming that at most $\\sim$2.4 pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch.",
          " ",
          "-Across all 57 MMLU subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\\% as a robustness check (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$.",
          "+Across all 57 MMLU subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\\% as a robustness check (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. Odds ratio regression reveals a Simpson's paradox: domain accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$ per 10 pp, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$), confirming that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$.",
```
