    "newString": "\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces baseline FPR below 31\\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 injection seeds (baseline row only).}\n\\label{tab:structural_comparison}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nApproach & Mechanism & Temp.\\ FPR & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\\ total score & $0.314_{\\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\\pm.027}$ & Reference \\\\\nMatched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\\ DIF & ${\\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\\ 0.94--1.04 \\\\\n$\\theta$-cond. & $\\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\\theta,\\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\\\\nWithin-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ ($>$25\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal item instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n",

    "newString": "A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. Cross-benchmark analysis of all six METABENCH benchmarks confirms universality: C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% to 31.3\\% (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones.",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), and find that 31\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\\%) of the instability; 92.4\\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation; cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

Cross-benchmark analysis of all six METABENCH benchmarks confirms universality: C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% to 31.3\\% (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.",
          "+LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\\%) of the instability; 92.4\\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. Cross-benchmark analysis of all six METABENCH benchmarks confirms universality: C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% to 31.3\\% (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about MMLU's measurement properties.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nThe MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale DIF characterization across six LLM benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), finding 17.9--31.3\\% temporal instability robust across thresholds and unrelated to accuracy gains (confirmed by Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression); (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\\Cref{sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about MMLU's measurement properties.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nThe MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale characterization of item-level benchmark stability at 5,227 models, finding 31\\% temporal instability that is robust across thresholds and unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains; (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification, with C\\% ranging from 21\\% to 48\\% across all 57 subjects (mean $\\approx 30\\%$) and confirmed non-random by random-split control at 0.18\\% (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\\Cref{sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

  "old_string": "The 31\\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$); ability-matched C\\% of $28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$ confirms the 31.3\\% rate is not inflated by impact confounding. Domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 pp of 31.3\\% C\\%; 92.4\\% is item-specific. This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.",
  "new_string": "The 31\\% ETS~C instability rate on MMLU is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Cross-benchmark analysis reveals this is not MMLU-specific: all six METABENCH benchmarks exhibit substantial temporal DIF (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). A task-type dichotomy---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) vs.\\ procedural benchmarks (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$)---suggests that knowledge-intensive items are more sensitive to training data composition shifts. Odds ratio regression confirms a Simpson's paradox: accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$), establishing that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ on MMLU) but signals measurement drift across model generations."
}

  "old_string": "Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), we find that 31\\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification. The instability is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$), confirmed non-random by a random-split control (0.18\\%), and predominantly item-specific (92.4\\%). This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift. Web-overlap contamination labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$), exposing a validation methodology gap; our semi-synthetic framework with cross-subject matching ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative, confirmed on GSM8K.",
  "new_string": "Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), we find that 17.9--31.3\\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\\%$). The instability is robust across thresholds, confirmed non-random by random-split controls ($\\leq 0.6\\%$), and predominantly item-specific---a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms that item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF classification. Web-overlap contamination labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$), exposing a validation methodology gap; our semi-synthetic framework with cross-subject matching ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DCNXiwmYXMZvkkrtNb5f9Z`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	The 31\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\% at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\Cref{sec:structural}). The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\%$); ability-matched C\% of $28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$ confirms the 31.3\% rate is not inflated by impact confounding. Domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 pp of 31.3\% C\%; 92.4\% is item-specific. This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.
8	
9	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
10	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
11	
12	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: the dominant validation practice of evaluating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items. This near-independence is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.
13	
14	\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}
15	\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}
16	
17	The GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization. Models with larger contamination gaps benefit \emph{less} from DIF-C items. Of the 249 DIF-C items on GSM8K, 171 favor the reference (2023) cohort ($\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} > 1$). Partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms the effect is not an artifact of general model quality. Caveats include a wide confidence interval (95\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$) and limited sample size.
18	
19	Item characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\times$ direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).
20	
21	Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.
22	
23	Structural constraints and limitations are discussed in the Limitations section.
24	
25	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
26	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
27	
28	These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\% $> 20\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).
29	
```

      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$); ability-matched C\\% of $28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$ confirms the 31.3\\% rate is not inflated by impact confounding. Domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 pp of 31.3\\% C\\%; 92.4\\% is item-specific. This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: the dominant validation practice of evaluating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items. This near-independence is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization. Models with larger contamination gaps benefit \\emph{less} from DIF-C items. Of the 249 DIF-C items on GSM8K, 171 favor the reference (2023) cohort ($\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} > 1$). Partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms the effect is not an artifact of general model quality. Caveats include a wide confidence interval (95\\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$) and limited sample size.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.\n\nStructural constraints and limitations are discussed in the Limitations section.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).\n",
      "numLines": 29,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 29
    }
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XzpsmbTX8koDQ5wtY2kuZz`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), we find that 31\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification. The instability is robust across thresholds (10--49\% at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in [2.5, 1.0]$), confirmed non-random by a random-split control (0.18\%), and predominantly item-specific (92.4\%). This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift. Web-overlap contamination labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\approx 0.96$), exposing a validation methodology gap; our semi-synthetic framework with cross-subject matching ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative, confirmed on GSM8K.
5	
6	DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal, Jaccard with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode. DIF is a diagnostic lens, not a quality filter. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring---periodic DIF audits, item-level stability labels, and cross-subject matching---alongside score reporting.
7	
```

</details>

{

  "old_string": "The 31\\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$); ability-matched C\\% of $28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$ confirms the 31.3\\% rate is not inflated by impact confounding. This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.",
  "new_string": "The 31\\% ETS~C instability rate on MMLU is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Cross-benchmark analysis reveals this is not MMLU-specific: all six METABENCH benchmarks exhibit substantial temporal DIF (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). A task-type dichotomy---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) vs.\\ procedural benchmarks (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$)---suggests that knowledge-intensive items are more sensitive to training data composition shifts. Odds ratio regression confirms a Simpson's paradox: accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$), establishing that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ on MMLU) but signals measurement drift across model generations."
}

The instability is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$), confirmed non-random by a random-split control (0.18\\%), and predominantly item-specific (92.4\\%). This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift. Web-overlap contamination labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$), exposing a validation methodology gap; our semi-synthetic framework with cross-subject matching ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative, confirmed on GSM8K.",
    "newString": "Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), we find that 17.9--31.3\\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\\%$). The instability is robust across thresholds, confirmed non-random by random-split controls ($\\leq 0.6\\%$), and predominantly item-specific---a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms that item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF classification.     "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nApplying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), we find that 31\\% of items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS Category~C classification. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015BHUyHxaymELLtQkR4ZDgV`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Methodological scope of MH-DIF.}
4	The Mantel-Haenszel procedure detects only \emph{uniform} DIF, where the odds ratio between cohorts is constant across ability strata. Logistic regression DIF analysis reveals an additional 24.8\% of items exhibiting non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), making the 31\% ETS~C estimate conservative. Future work should apply non-uniform DIF methods (e.g., logistic regression or IRT-based approaches) as the primary analysis to capture the full spectrum of item instability. Critically, MH-DIF is nonparametric and conditions on observed total score, not latent $\theta$---its validity does not depend on IRT model fit~\citep{holland1988mh}. Our Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence domains do not correlate with DIF domains ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), further supporting the robustness of the MH procedure in this setting.
5	
6	\paragraph{Ability confounding.}
7	Although the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible (Cohen's $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\%$) and nearest-neighbor matching confirms that at most 2.4 percentage points of C\% are attributable to ability mismatch, within-family comparisons with large ability gaps (e.g., Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3, $d = -0.99$) produce substantially inflated C\% (61.3\% unmatched vs.\ 28.3\% matched). Any application of MH-DIF to cohorts with larger ability differences must incorporate ability matching or stratification beyond the standard 5-strata procedure.
8	
9	\paragraph{Sample size requirements.}
10	Our power analysis indicates reliable ETS~C detection requires $N \geq 80$ per cohort. The temporal cohorts (1,080 and 807 models) far exceed this threshold, but the GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power (95\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$). The negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$) is exploratory and warrants replication with larger model samples.
11	
12	\paragraph{Contamination vs.\ capability change.}
13	DIF analysis detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts but cannot determine \emph{why}. The differential signal may reflect contamination, capability change, training methodology shifts, or interactions among these factors. We present evidence that DIF captures something distinct from memorization (negative GSM1K correlation, semi-synthetic dose-response), but a definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data.
14	
15	\paragraph{Benchmark scope.}
16	We analyze MMLU (12,508 items) and GSM8K (1,319 items), both multiple-choice or short-answer benchmarks scored as binary correct/incorrect. The generalizability of our findings to open-ended generation benchmarks (e.g., AlpacaEval, MT-Bench), coding benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval), or benchmarks with non-binary scoring remains untested. The binary response format is a prerequisite for MH-DIF; extending DIF analysis to continuous or partial-credit scoring would require ordinal or polytomous DIF methods.
17	
18	\paragraph{Temporal cohort definition.}
19	We define cohorts by calendar year of model release (2023 vs.\ 2024), a coarse grouping that aggregates models with diverse training pipelines, data mixtures, and post-training procedures. Finer-grained cohort definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff, model family, or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns of item instability but would reduce per-cohort sample sizes and statistical power.
20	
```

</details>

The generalizability of our findings to open-ended generation benchmarks (e.g., AlpacaEval, MT-Bench), coding benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval), or benchmarks with non-binary scoring remains untested. The binary response format is a prerequisite for MH-DIF; extending DIF analysis to continuous or partial-credit scoring would require ordinal or polytomous DIF methods.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Benchmark scope.}\nWe analyze six METABENCH benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande, TruthfulQA, ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag; 27,125 total items), all multiple-choice or short-answer benchmarks scored as binary correct/incorrect. The generalizability to open-ended generation benchmarks (e.g., AlpacaEval, MT-Bench), coding benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval), or benchmarks with non-binary scoring remains untested. The binary response format is a prerequisite for MH-DIF; extending to continuous or partial-credit scoring would require polytomous DIF methods.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological scope of MH-DIF.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure detects only \\emph{uniform} DIF, where the odds ratio between cohorts is constant across ability strata. Logistic regression DIF analysis reveals an additional 24.8\\% of items exhibiting non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% ETS~C estimate conservative. Future work should apply non-uniform DIF methods (e.g., logistic regression or IRT-based approaches) as the primary analysis to capture the full spectrum of item instability. Critically, MH-DIF is nonparametric and conditions on observed total score, not latent $\\theta$---its validity does not depend on IRT model fit~\\citep{holland1988mh}. Our Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence domains do not correlate with DIF domains ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), further supporting the robustness of the MH procedure in this setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Ability confounding.}\nAlthough the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible (Cohen's $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$) and nearest-neighbor matching confirms that at most 2.4 percentage points of C\\% are attributable to ability mismatch, within-family comparisons with large ability gaps (e.g., Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3, $d = -0.99$) produce substantially inflated C\\% (61.3\\% unmatched vs.\\ 28.3\\% matched). Any application of MH-DIF to cohorts with larger ability differences must incorporate ability matching or stratification beyond the standard 5-strata procedure.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size requirements.}\nOur power analysis indicates reliable ETS~C detection requires $N \\geq 80$ per cohort. The temporal cohorts (1,080 and 807 models) far exceed this threshold, but the GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power (95\\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$). The negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$) is exploratory and warrants replication with larger model samples.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination vs.\\ capability change.}\nDIF analysis detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts but cannot determine \\emph{why}. The differential signal may reflect contamination, capability change, training methodology shifts, or interactions among these factors. We present evidence that DIF captures something distinct from memorization (negative GSM1K correlation, semi-synthetic dose-response), but a definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data.\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark scope.}\nWe analyze MMLU (12,508 items) and GSM8K (1,319 items), both multiple-choice or short-answer benchmarks scored as binary correct/incorrect. The generalizability of our findings to open-ended generation benchmarks (e.g., AlpacaEval, MT-Bench), coding benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval), or benchmarks with non-binary scoring remains untested. The binary response format is a prerequisite for MH-DIF; extending DIF analysis to continuous or partial-credit scoring would require ordinal or polytomous DIF methods.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal cohort definition.}\nWe define cohorts by calendar year of model release (2023 vs.\\ 2024), a coarse grouping that aggregates models with diverse training pipelines, data mixtures, and post-training procedures. Finer-grained cohort definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff, model family, or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns of item instability but would reduce per-cohort sample sizes and statistical power.\n",

  "old_string": "The 31\\% ETS~C instability rate on MMLU is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Cross-benchmark analysis reveals this is not MMLU-specific: all six METABENCH benchmarks exhibit substantial temporal DIF (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). A task-type dichotomy---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) vs.\\ procedural benchmarks (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$)---suggests that knowledge-intensive items are more sensitive to training data composition shifts. Odds ratio regression confirms a Simpson's paradox: accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$), establishing that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ on MMLU) but signals measurement drift across model generations.",

    "oldString": "The 31\\% ETS~C instability rate on MMLU is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability rate on MMLU is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Cross-benchmark analysis reveals this is not MMLU-specific: all six METABENCH benchmarks exhibit substantial temporal DIF (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). A task-type dichotomy---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) vs.\\ procedural benchmarks (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$)---suggests that knowledge-intensive items are more sensitive to training data composition shifts. Odds ratio regression confirms a Simpson's paradox: accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$), establishing that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ on MMLU) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: the dominant validation practice of evaluating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items. This near-independence is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization. Models with larger contamination gaps benefit \\emph{less} from DIF-C items. Of the 249 DIF-C items on GSM8K, 171 favor the reference (2023) cohort ($\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} > 1$). Partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms the effect is not an artifact of general model quality. Caveats include a wide confidence interval (95\\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$) and limited sample size.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.\n\nStructural constraints and limitations are discussed in the Limitations section.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).\n",
